#210. Iran war: Gulf tanker attacked, 1 seaferer killed

Iran is stepping up attacks on shipping in the Persian Gulf and Strait of Hormuz. A Greek-owned tanker believed to have been struck with a missile, and Iran said two other vessels were on fire.

An explosion believed to have been caused by a missile attack on ‌an oil ⁠tanker in the Persian Gulf left one seafarer dead, the vessel's operating company said on Friday.

Greek management firm Velos Tankers said the ​Marshall Islands-flagged tanker MT Velos Sapphire ​was sailing towards Bahrain when it ‌was attacked.

"The company is ‌greatly saddened to report the ‌death ​of one of its seafarers, who was found fatally injured on board after having initially been ​reported missing in the aftermath of the explosion," the firm said.

The explosion caused a fire to break out in the ship. The crew were able to extinguish the fire without reporting any other injured.

Bahrain's Khalifa Bin Salman Port ultimately took in the ship and now a investigation into the incident will be launched to assess the extent of the damage to the vessel's hull.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The incident is the latest in a string of attacks on ships in the Persian Gulf by Iran.

US newspaper The Wall Street Journal (WSJ) cited regional officials, security firms and analysts in a report detailing how Iran is using everything it can to stop the flow of oil leaving the Strait of Hormuz.

According to the report, Tehran has launched bomb-laden undersea drones, fired rocket-propelled grenades and deployed crews to attach limpet mines to ships over recent weeks.

According to data from the UK Maritime Trade Operations Center (UKMTO), October saw the highest monthly number of attacks in the Strait of Hormuz since the war began, with more than 35 incidents.

At least five vessels have been attacked so far in November, UKMTO added.

The increasing attacks coincides with signs of recovery in shipping through Hormuz. US Central Command said last month it had helped Gulf countries export 1.5 billion barrels of oil via Hormuz since the summer.

On Thursday, Iranian media reported that five oil tankers and a tugboat caught fire after hitting mines in the Strait of Hormuz.

On Friday, Iran's Revolutionary Guards said two more oil tankers were on fire in the strait, state media reported.

Meanwhile, Iranian media said ​on Friday ​that Iranian ​air defenses ‌had destroyed a US ‌MQ-1 drone ‌over Qeshm Island in the Strait ​of Hormuz.

The ‌United States did not confirm the incident.

It all comes in the wake of the US launching strikes on Qeshm Island on Tuesday, with Iranian state media reporting five explosions in rapid succession.

A statement attributed to Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei warned that the Middle East war would widen if the US resumes its attacks.

Khamenei  has rejected the notion that Washington will change course after midterm elections dealt a blow to President Donald Trump.

#209. Fox completes its $22B acquisition of Roku

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Fox has completed its $22 billion acquisition of Roku, wrapping up one of the biggest media deals in recent years and giving the company a major boost in the streaming and connected TV space.

Announced Friday, the deal brings the streaming company founded in 2002 formally under Fox’s ownership. Fox says the combined company now ranks as the third-largest television business in the U.S. by viewership.

“We started Roku more than two decades ago to give people a better way to watch TV, and that mission doesn’t change today. What does change is our reach and resources,” Roku founder Anthony Wood said in a statement. “Our teams will keep building and innovating as they always have, now with the support and capabilities of Fox behind us.”

For Fox, the move provides direct access to Roku’s reach of more than 100 million households. The company expects that audience to help it improve advertising targeting, strengthen its position in the fast-growing connected TV market, and reduce its reliance on traditional TV distribution.

Today’s announcement comes two months after the Justice Department issued a second request for information, asking both companies to provide additional data and documents as part of its antitrust review. Earlier this month, reports suggested the deal was nearing U.S. regulatory approval.

The acquisition also closed much earlier than expected. When the deal was first announced in June, the companies projected it would be completed in the first half of 2027.

#208. Democrats blast Trump’s decision to make Stephen Miller his chief of staff as president defends picking loyal ‘MVP’ - as it happened

Stephen Miller, architect of Trump’s immigration crackdown, to become White House chief of staff

Stephen Miller, one of Donald Trump’s fiercest allies and architect of the administration’s hardline deportation agenda, will now be the White House chief of staff. The role is a promotion for Miller, who currently serves as an adviser. Trump said that Miller will start the job on Monday 9 November, less than a week after Republicans’ crushing defeat in the midterm elections.

Wiles will now take a role in Trump’s political operation, which includes his Super Pac Maga Inc. On social media, she said that serving the president has been “the honor of a lifetime”. Wiles has been in the president’s orbit for over a decade, and prior to her role at the White House, she managed, alongside Chris LaCivita, Trump’s winning 2024 presidential campaign.

Trump later told reporters that he decided to promote Miller because he “wanted to make a change”. He praised Wiles, and said that Miller had been by his side “from the beginning”. “Sometimes in life you have to make moves,” the president said.

Meanwhile, Democrats blasted Trump’s decision, after Republicans faced a drubbing in the midterm elections. Chuck Schumer, the Senate minority leader, said that the president “learned nothing from his historic defeat”, and Congressman Robert Garcia – poised to be the chair of the House oversight committee – advised Miller to “lawyer up”. Garcia said that in the majority, Democrats on his panel will “continue to investigate [Stephen] Miller for his misconduct, and we will fight against his hateful policies in Congress and the courts”.

This concludes our live coverage of the second and final Trump administration at the end of a momentous week. We leave you with the news:

The elevation of Stephen Miller, the architect of Donald Trump’s brutal and unpopular mass deportation agenda, to White House chief of staff suggests that the president has no intention of rolling back the crackdown, even after voters delivered a sweeping rebuke by electing Democrats to every House seat along the US border with Mexico.

While Trump’s hardline supporters were thrilled, there was bipartisan dismay at the news. “Stephen Miller belongs nowhere near the Oval Office,”the NAACP president said. “He belongs before a judge. He’s a thug who will be remembered as the architect of Trump’s vile and authoritarian regime.”

Republican congressman Marlin Stutzman, who said on Wednesday, “I think Stephen Miller should be fired this morning”, told Semafor appointing Miller was a “horrible decision.”

Senator Chris Murphy was released from a hospital on Friday following treatment for a stroke and plans to spend several weeks at a rehabilitation center as he works to recover, the Connecticut Democrat said in a statement.

Multiple reporters said that the phrase “wartime consigliere” had been used by the White House to describe Miller, which is a reference to what a close adviser to mafia boss is called in The Godfather. The phrase has been used repeatedly in recent years by people close to Trump to describe what he is looking for in a right-hand man, who will use slash-and-burn tactics to defend him at all costs.

As social networks continue to fill with anger at the selection of Stephen Miller as White House chief of staff, the White House released a taxpayer-funded ad to promote the Trump aide, in the form of a hype video starring the man himself posted on official government social media accounts.

The Associated Press reports that the US secret service said a suspect has been taken into custody and charged after officers found him in a vehicle near the White House with multiple guns, claiming he heard voices in his head telling him to kill Donald Trump.

The arrest Thursday night happened after secret service officers approached a man “acting suspicious” in a parked car, according to a Homeland Security statement. US officials said the suspect – identified as Ronny Yordrix Guanipa Chrinos – went on to describe voices in his head that were telling him to come to the nation’s capital and kill the Republican president.

Progressive Democrat Aisha Wahab has been re-elected to Congress to represent California’s 14th congressional district, defeating fellow Democrat Melissa Hernandez, for a House seat formerly held by Eric Swalwell, who resigned in April over sexual misconduct allegations he has denied.

Wahab, a former California state senator, previously won a special election to serve out the rest of Swalwell’s term in August, when she also beat Hernandez, the president of the board of directors for the Bay Area Rapid Transit agency.

After his speech in Florida on Friday, a reporter asked Donald Trump about a phrase White House officials are using to explain his choice of Stephen Miller as his new chief of staff.

“Mr President, there’s people describing Stephen Miller as your ‘wartime consigliere’ – do you agree with that characterization?” the reporter asked.

“Well, he’s a great fighter,” Trump replied. “He’s been with me right from the beginning, 2015, he was with me from before 2016, 2015, and he’s very loyal, smart, does a great job. He’s been an MVP every year. He’s been just great. So we’re very happy with him – and he’s gotten great reviews.”

That final remark was perhaps an indication of just how cloistered the president is, given that there has been widespread anger at dismay at his choice of Miller from Democrats and some Republicans, but the White House issued a press release on the appointment which claimed “The news was met with widespread praise”, followed by quotes lauding Miller from five dozen current and former administration officials, Republican lawmakers and pundits.

Multiple reporters said that the phrase “wartime consigliere” had been used to describe Miller by White House sources on Friday, which is a reference to what a close adviser to mafia boss is called in The Godfather.

The phrase has been used repeatedly in recent years by people close to Trump to describe what he is looking for in a right-hand man, who will use slash-and-burn tactics to defend him at all costs.

In 2020, Matt Gaetz, a Republican congressman from Florida at the time who was later Trump’s first choice to be attorney general in his second term, told the New York Times that Trump’s choice of Mark Meadows as his fourth White House chief of staff was “a great move” since “he’s a great wartime consigliere and we’re heading into election season.”

In 2023, as Trump faced multiple criminal investigations, Steve Bannon described his legal adviser Boris Epshteyn as “a wartime consigliere.”

The role Trump seems to be looking to fill is one that was first played in his life by Roy Cohn, his former personal lawyer and fixer, who had been a top aide to senator Joseph McCarthy during investigations into supposed communist infiltration of the US government in the 1950s.

It was Cohn who had first advised Trump in 1973 to respond to the federal government’s allegation that Trump and his father had violated laws against racial discrimination in housing by refusing to rent to Black tenants, by going on the offensive and countersuing the government for $100bn.

In 2017, when the support for Trump’s campaign from the Russian government was under investigation, Trump was enraged that his first attorney general, Jeff Sessions, decided to recuse himself instead of undermining the probe. “Where’s my Roy Cohn?” the president reportedly asked aides.

On Tuesday, voters resoundingly rebuked Donald Trump and his brutal mass deportation campaign that sent military tanks and masked agents though residential streets.

But on Friday, the president doubled down. He named Stephen Miller – the architect of the administration’s most aggressive immigration policies – as his chief of staff.

It was never likely that Trump – who rose to political prominence on the promise that he would expel immigrants en masse from the US – would dampen his agenda, even after voters rejected Republican candidates up and down the ballot, near and far from the southern border, in blue and deep-red states.

But the elevation of Miller – a zealous, anti-immigrant crusader who has been by Trump’s side since the president’s first term – is an ominous signal that the deportation machine that Trump has built up over the past two years will be driving ahead at full speed.

Miller more than any single other person is responsible for unleashing a series of violent immigration raids across US cities this year and last, for sending hordes of masked agents in military gear through residential streets to round up undocumented immigrants. He has pushed the administration to all but shut down legal immigration as well. In his previous roles – as a deputy chief of staff and policy adviser – he has stuck close to Trump’s side and stoked the president’s most extreme instincts.

“The elevation of Stephen Miller to White House chief of staff signals a doubling down on the administration’s cruelest impulses,” said Faisal Al-Juburi, the co-chief executive of Raíces, a Texas-based humanitarian aid and immigration services non-profit. “Miller’s promotion guarantees an acceleration of mass deportations and of brute force unleashed within our neighborhoods.”

After immigration agents shot and killed Alex Pretti, a nurse, amid immigration raids in Minneapolis, it was Miller, according to the Wall Street Journal, who raised the idea that Trump could invoke the Insurrection Act to send in military troops.

The US Department of Justice moved on Friday to dismiss the lawsuit it brought against the state of California, in which it sought to keep roughly 20,000 ballots it alleged went to noncitizens from being counted.

The supreme court ruled against the Trump administration on Monday, after several lower courts sided with California officials, who argued the federal government was seeking to interfere in state-run elections.

The request to dismiss the case comes three days after the final votes were cast in the midterm elections and about a week before a scheduled hearing where a special master would have been asked to evaluate the accuracy of the list provided by the federal government.

California officials argued that the list is out of date and includes legitimate voters — a sentiment that the federal judge presiding over the case echoed in his blistering order denying the federal government’s request.

Democrat Nancy Lacore won her party’s 254th seat in the House, defeating Republican Jenny Costa Honeycutt in the race to replace Republican Nancy Mace in representing South Carolina’s first congressional district.

Lacore is a former Navy vice admiral who served as chief of the Navy Reserve before she was fired last year without explanation by the former Fox News weekend host Donald Trump chose as defense secretary, Pete Hegseth.

Lacore’s victory ensures that the Democrats will have at least 254 seats in the next Congress, with 19 races still to be called, nine of which are led by Democrats. Republican have currently won 162 seats.

Mace chose to run for governor instead of re-election to the House, but finished a distant fifth in the Republican primary in June before starting a podcast, Get Maced, that she recently used to promote the racist conspiracy theory that “Radical Islam Is CONQUERING America From Within”.

Reading aloud from prepared remarks to NASA officials at Cape Canaveral in Florida on Friday, Donald Trump ran through a list of what he called evidence that Americans were “the greatest pioneers explorers, inventors and innovators who ever lived.”

“America is the nation that dreamed and created the modern world,” Trump said, peering at a teleprompter from beneath a USA cap. “We laid the railroads, harnessed electricity and invented the lightbulb, the telephone, the airplane, the assembly line, the television, the microchip, the personal computer, the internet, GPS, the smartphone, and almost every other thing- think of it – almost every other thing that’s worth mentioning.”

He then looked out at the audience and veered off-script to comment: “That’s a cool list, though, when you think, huh? How about a little piece of each of them? Wouldn’t that be nice? You wouldn’t be sitting here right now. You’d be someplace else.”

The president then returned to his prepared remarks, looking back at the teleprompter and saying: “We charted the human genome to cure diseases,” but stumbled badly over the word “genome” which he pronounced “guh-nome”.

Despite Trump’s attempt to claim that the US deserves credit for sequencing all the DNA in human chromosomes, the Human Genome Project was, in fact, the work of an international network of geneticists.

The president made the flub minutes after boasting about the fact that his uncle, John Trump, had been a long-tenured professor at MIT. Trump has repeatedly claimed that his uncle’s intelligence and scientific knowledge was passed on to him through his genes, giving him an innate understanding of complex scientific questions, from the novel coronavirus that caused a global pandemic in 2020 to nuclear weapons.

Donald Trump, remarkably, gave yet another speech on Friday, delivering remarks at the John F. Kennedy Space Center in Cape Canaveral, Florida in his official capacity as president, which were, nonetheless, threaded through with partisan political talking points.

The president emerged to the theme tune from his rallies, God Bless the USA, and mimed dancing, before starting his speech by celebrating the victories of two Florida Republicans, Byron Donalds, the governor-elect, and senator Ashley Moody.

The pair were the only two candidates Trump rallied for in the past month who won competitive races.

He then praised members of his administration who were present, singling out the US ambassador to the United Nations, Mike Waltz, for “the way you took on, the Cubans”. Waltz was recently engaged in a spat with a Cuban official at the UN which played on heavy rotation on Fox News, the president’s window on the world.

Trump then suggested that he was glad that the US stock market plunged after the Republican wipeout at the ballot box, on fears that companies building AI data centers might not be as free to push ahead with regulation by a Democratic Congress.

“Yesterday, you know, our country lost $3 trillion, all because of an election,” Trump said. “If we were to gain $3 trillion, I probably would have been more unhappy. But you know what that means; that means that they thought somebody else did well in an election.”

“By the way, I was not on the ballot, just in case you had any question,” he added. “Because if we were on the ballot, we would’ve won but we weren’t.”

Senator Chris Murphy was released from a hospital on Friday following treatment for a stroke and plans to spend several weeks at a rehabilitation center as he works to recover, the Connecticut Democrat said in a statement.

“Thank you for the outpouring of support my family and I have received over the last few days,” Murphy said. “I want to be direct with you, so here is my diagnosis. Last Saturday afternoon, I felt something was not right with my body and called 9-1-1 out of an abundance of caution, and I’m glad I did. I was diagnosed with a small bleed in the thalamus region of my brain.”

“The doctors are very encouraged by the progress I’ve made so far. In order to take my recovery seriously and get back to work on the quickest timeline possible, I will be spending several weeks at a rehabilitation center after being discharged from the hospital today,” he continued.

The senator also released a statement from Dr Jose Suarez, the chief of neurosciences critical care at the Johns Hopkins Hospital. “Senator Murphy experienced a small hemorrhagic stroke. He has shown tremendous progress, and at the current rate of improvement, we expect that he will make a full recovery following rehabilitation,” the doctor said.

The morning after the midterm wave that swept away dozens of his colleagues, congressman Marlin Stutzman, an Indiana Republican who held his seat, told voters that Donald Trump’s mass deportation agenda was a major factor, and called on the president to fire its architect, Stephen Miller.

“The press ran with it, the Democrats ran with it, and it looked bad on us – the deportation effort,” Stutzman said on Wednesday. “President Obama deported more illegals than what President Trump has. But you know what? he didn’t he didn’t go out and talk about it the way that, you know, we did. I mean, frankly, I think Stephen Miller should be fired this morning. I mean, there needs to be some changs at hte White House.”

Asked by Semafor on Friday what he made of Trump’s decision to not fire but promote Miller, Stutzman told the outlet it was a “horrible decision.”

Several newly elected Democrats and Senator Bernie Sanders have renewed calls for datacenter moratoriums in the days after the US midterm elections. The sweeping Democratic victory, which was buoyed in part by backlash to the tech industry, has added momentum to efforts to slow down the multi-trillion-dollar buildout of the controversial facilities.

On the social media platform X, Sanders posted a call for an immediate national datacenter moratorium this week. “Americans don’t want Big Tech oligarchs wrecking the environment, gutting jobs and threatening humanity’s future,” Sanders said on Wednesday.

Sanders was joined by several Democrats, including Representative Yassamin Ansari of Arizona – a state where Elon Musk’s SpaceX has proposed a datacenter project that would span more than 700 acres.

“Top of the Democratic agenda on day 1 needs to be a pause on advanced AI development, regulation of this out of control industry, and a ban on artificial superintelligence,” Ansari posted Wednesday on X. Musk mocked her post in a retweet, and the two sparred, with Ansari forecasting congressional hearings, telling the centi-billionaire: “See you soon.”

The pushback to the buildout of datacenters, which tech companies need to power the AI products that have become central to their businesses, was one of the most visible issues of the midterms. Opposition to datacenters figured heavily in campaigns across the US, while voters passed dozens of local ballot measures aimed at restricting datacenter projects in their states.

#207. Pentagon offers battery startup 6K $250M loan to build US factory

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Batteries are everywhere, even on the battlefield, which helps explain the Pentagon’s recent interest in building more domestic manufacturing capacity.

The 12-year-old, North Andover, Massachusetts-based startup 6K announced Thursday that it won conditional approval for a $250 million loan from the Department of Defense to build a factory in Tennessee. The plant will use a unique microwave-powered process to produce battery materials that don’t rely on Chinese suppliers.

The company previously received a $50 million grant from the Department of Energy under the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law, though that 2023 grant was canceled by the Trump administration in a chaotic sweep of Biden-era awards, forcing 6K to reapply.

The factory was originally slated to open in 2024, but the grant cancelation and uncertainties in the EV market forced the company to rejigger its plans.

6K’s manufacturing process uses microwaves to heat metals into a plasma and then deposit them onto a surface, reducing the number of steps required to make cathode material, a key battery component. The company says the process can work on a number of different battery types, including lithium iron phosphate (LFP).

#203. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#202. Want to have a better chat? Scientists say the secret is ‘pink noise’

The most enjoyable conversations strike a balance between exploring a topic and pulling in fresh ideas, study finds

As an early stab at rules for conversation, Cicero did a fine job: watch your tone, never look bored, never lose your temper and never ramble on, particularly about yourself, he advised.

Now scientists have followed in the great orator’s footsteps and discovered a telltale signature of an enjoyable chinwag. The secret of a mutually satisfying exchange is a healthy dose of pink noise, they say.

The colour of noise tends to animate physicists more than linguists, but researchers at Dartmouth College in New Hampshire in the US found the concept provided fresh insights into what makes a good conversation.

Kathryn O’Nell, the first author on the study, said: “In physics, people talk about pink noise as existing on the edge of chaos, where you can stay at a stable point, but you’re not stuck there for ever.

“It’s particularly cool for conversation, because you can stay somewhere long enough to really get to know each other, to understand the topic, but you’re not stuck there while it gets stale.”

The researchers analysed more than 1,000 conversations between strangers, who answered questions on the chats afterwards. People enjoyed conversations more, wanted to continue chatting, and felt a stronger sense of connection when the tête-à-têtes exhibited more pink noise, they found.

To see whether the effect applied beyond strangers, the researchers ran a similar analysis on conversations between friends about their formative experiences. Again, more pink noise meant more enjoyment.

The effect had real-world consequences, too. When the scientists turned to speed-dating conversations, they found that more pink noise predicted which pairs met up for a second date. Details have been published in Science Advances.

Nailing down what makes a great chat is notoriously tricky, the researchers said. In a bold opening statement to their paper, the authors write: “Good conversation is like pornography – it is difficult to define, but we know it when we see it.”

Colloquially, people talk about being in sync, but the metaphor misses something, said Dr Emily Finn, a neuroscientist and senior author on the paper. “In sync can be incredibly dull,” she said. People can be in sync when they repeat each other’s words and circle a single subject. They might well be immensely bored.

In the study, the scientists took conversations and fed each side into an AI called MPNet. The program scored every turn of the chat on 768 different criteria, covering aspects such as meaning, tone and context. This allowed the scientists to quantify how conversations ebbed and flowed over time.

Conversations exhibit white noise when people fail to follow what has just been said: the chat might jump all over the place, or be dominated by a person who seems oblivious to the other person’s responses. Other conversations can have red noise, which arises when people mirror each other’s language and fall into a conversational rut.

Pink noise lies somewhere in between: a conversational Goldilocks zone that balances stability and flexibility. People will synchronise as they explore a topic, then drift apart as they pull in fresh ideas before converging again to explore those.

The pinkiness of a conversation depends on the people involved, but O’Nell said she suspected the skill could be learned and developed. As part of the study, the team analysed podcasts fronted by two hosts and found that the pinkiness of their conversations increased over the first 50 episodes.

Prof Elizabeth Stokoe, a conversation analyst at the London School of Economics, praised the researchers for moving beyond the idea that “more similarity is better”. But asking whether a conversation is enjoyable risks missing the point, she added.

“There’s a lovely study of a phone call in which a father and his daughter, a young woman with learning disabilities living in residential care, take a long time to say goodbye, and the ending is painful and poignant,” she said. “Reducing conversations to whether they are ‘enjoyable’ can feel bloodless, since many of the conversations that matter most in our lives are like this.”

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Meta recently launched its Muse agents — a new generation of cuddly agentic helpers. Now, the company has launched Spaces, a new way to communicate and collaborate with them.

As far as I can tell, Spaces are basically breakout conversations that revolve around a particular topic — although Meta refers to them as “custom experiences.” They live in the sidebar of the interface where you interact with your Muse agent. Clicking on the Space brings up the conversation, where Muse works toward a particular goal on your behalf.

For instance, if you wanted to plan a vacation, you could open a new Space focused on that particular project, and whenever you want to work on it with Muse, you would click to open that particular tab. Or, if you’re focused on collecting recipes for an upcoming family event, you’d open a new Space for that particular project. Other examples Meta gives are a holiday gift tracker, a wedding planner, or a space for DIY projects.

“Describe an idea in chat, and Muse builds a space for you, whether that’s a tracker, a planner or a project board,” Meta says.

Spaces can also be easily integrated with third-party apps and information, Meta says.

“Connect the apps you use, and your spaces keep themselves up to date in the background, so new concert dates, price changes, or savings progress show up without you having to ask,” the company says.

The company is obviously busy building out the user experience around the agents. Spaces is actually the second new Muse feature that Meta has debuted over the last week. On Thursday, the company also announced Muse Voice, which allows users to video chat with their agent and give it commands verbally.

The company is in competition with other cute and cuddly AI agents — including OpenAI’s dots and SpaceXAI’s Grokbot, which were debuted with similarly cartoonish personas.

Thomas Markle Sr. was charged with one count of possession of child sex abuse imagery, prosecutors said in a statement.

If convicted, Thomas Markle Sr. would face a statutory maximum sentence of 20 years in federal prison.

Her father did not attend their wedding, prompting extensive coverage in the British media of Meghan's estrangement from her father.

Thomas Markle Sr., a retried television lighting and photography director, was living in the Philippines earlier this year when he suffered a stroke and had his left leg amputated.

"I have supported, cared for him and helped him for years — but that stops now," Markle Jr. told The Sun. "I won't speak to my father ever again unless I’m called to give evidence against him."

#198. Hundreds of Artex AI hacking tools found publicly exposed to the web

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Security researchers have discovered hundreds of copies of a Chinese-made AI hacking tool publicly exposed to the internet for anyone to access, potentially allowing outsiders to launch cyberattacks against unsuspecting targets.

The hacking software, called Artex, is an open source autonomous penetration testing tool, meaning software that automatically probes computer systems for weaknesses, and it’s been linked to several cyberattacks since its public release in July. As open source software, it’s free for anyone to download and run on their own servers, making it a popular tool for those who cannot access Anthropic or OpenAI’s most advanced cybersecurity AI models for discovering security vulnerabilities. Microsoft has previously described Artex as a system of AI agents that can conduct cyberattacks “from reconnaissance through data exfiltration with little needed human interaction.”

In a blog post, cybersecurity firm Censys, which scans the internet for exposed or vulnerable systems, said that it identified more than 800 distinct deployments of Artex that were accessible from the web without needing a password to log in.

Silas Cutler, principal security researcher at Censys who wrote the blog post, said that not every instance of Artex is operated maliciously and many are used for authorized testers. But it’s unclear why hundreds of these Artex systems are exposed to the web, as the software does not require them to be accessible from the internet.

The findings underscore how even powerful hacking tools can be misconfigured to be publicly accessible by anyone, increasing the chance that someone will find and misuse them. The publicly accessible systems also expose attack tools, such as implants (hidden malicious software) used to compromise and maintain persistent access to a victim’s network.

Security companies have linked the use of Artex to several broad-scale cyberattacks, including against the Korean finance industry in September. Cybersecurity giant CrowdStrike attributed the attacks to a Chinese-speaking hacker who used an Artex instance in the attacks, in part because the hacker had left an “open directory” hosting the system. Censys said that its data showed the Artex instance in question had been publicly exposed for months, and appeared to contain more attack data that extended “well beyond South Korean banks,” including payment and gaming companies.

Artex was also used in an attack on IT support giant ConnectWise earlier in October, which allowed Chinese-linked hackers to spy on thousands of organizations whose data and files are hosted in Microsoft’s cloud. Microsoft, which has visibility into the attack, said in a blog post that the hackers used Artex to break into customers’ cloud environments and steal their data at such speed that defenders had “little time to detect and respond.”

Many of the attacks linked to Artex involved exploiting simple vulnerabilities, such as guessing weak or known passwords, and a lack of multi-factor authentication.

The findings show that simple vulnerabilities, including misconfigurations, can expose both the attacker and their targets.

Artex has since announced it would go closed-source, effectively limiting further releases for paid customers. There are still dozens of copies of Artex’s code available on code-sharing sites that can still be used in active attacks.

#196. Russell Brand barrister reports himself to watchdog over clash with journalist

Ian Winter KC told Rosamund Urwin of the Sunday Times, whose investigation led to Brand’s victims coming forward: ‘I hope you are pleased with yourself’

Russell Brand’s barrister has reported himself to his professional regulator after being accused of launching a tirade at a journalist shortly after his client had been found guilty of rape.

Ian Winter KC “confronted” the Sunday Times journalist Rosamund Urwin, whose investigation led to Brand’s victims coming forward, telling her: “I hope you are pleased with yourself.”

Urwin responded: “I’m pleased justice has been done,” to which Winter replied: “Well, it wasn’t. You fucked it.”

“Congratulations,” he added, in an incident that happened outside the courtroom door at Southwark crown court on Thursday afternoon, with a group of journalists watching.

In a statement issued on Friday lunchtime, Winter said he had referred himself to the Bar Standards Board, his professional regulator.

He said: “Having seen the ways matters have been characterised in today’s press, I consider my professional duty requires me to self-report those allegations to the Bar Standards Board so the matter can be subject to proper scrutiny.

The incident happened moments after a jury had found Brand guilty of rape and three counts of sexual assault at the conclusion of a four-week trial. He was cleared of two further charges of rape and one of attempted indecent assault.

Brand, 51, had denied all the charges against him, and argued through his lawyer that he had been wrongly accused and was the victim of a media conspiracy.

After the trial, experts added to calls for a change in the law to ensure juries are warned against “rape myths”.

In his closing speech, Winter said none of the complainants had “resisted or screamed or even tried to leave” during their encounters with Brand.

Claire Waxman, who is the victims’ commissioner for England and Wales, is seeking to update the law to ensure juries are warned against “rape myths”. She criticised “outdated and harmful assumptions” that victims must have screamed or tried to fight off their attacker.

Deniz Ugur, deputy director of the End Violence Against Women (EVAW) coalition, said: “It is really appalling to see defence lawyers intentionally lean into a whole range of tired tropes and myths about rape. “Rape myths like the ones we’ve seen in this case have no place in the courtroom.”

The head of Scotland Yard, Sir Mark Rowley, told BBC Radio 4’s Today programme he supported Waxman’s call. “I think what I’ve heard reported heads towards, it’s getting very close to victim-blaming, which is something we’ve thought very hard about in policing and make sure we don’t do.

“People fall into myths about [how] a rape victim must behave in a certain way, must react in a certain way. The evidence is really clear. Some people will fight. Some people will freeze. Some people will tell people straight away. Some people bottle it up for years. There isn’t one way of reacting.”

On Friday a No 10 source said the prime minister, Andy Burnham, believed that Waxman’s calls should be examined.

Speaking after the trial Urwin told a Times podcast she approached Winter after the verdict because her name had been mentioned during the trial. “I thought he’s talked about me a lot, I’ll go and introduce myself and that is a legitimate thing to do,” she said. “I was surprised to be confronted and spoken to in that way.”

It is understood that several complaints have been submitted to the Bar Standards Board but the regulator said it would not “comment as to whether or not it has received any information about potential misconduct by a barrister regardless of how any information comes to our attention”.

A spokesperson said complaints were usually handled confidentially unless they resulted in a disciplinary tribunal hearing.

The comedian Katherine Ryan criticised the barrister on her podcast, Telling Everybody Everything, saying: “That was so unprofessional, so inappropriate, so aggressive. Really sickening.”

Ben Taylor, the editor of the Sunday Times, defended Urwin, his newspaper’s chief news correspondent. He said: “I found what Mr Winter did quite extraordinary. I have never seen or heard of a barrister behaving in this way.

“There’s a really serious point here, which is that it was not Ros who found Russell Brand guilty at Southwark crown court.

“It was a jury of eight men and four women who have sat through this case. They’ve listened to the evidence, they’ve listened to the defence, and they have gone away and they’ve come back with some very considered verdicts.

“By impugning Ros’s integrity, what Mr Winter is doing, and he should really be called to account for this, he’s impugning the integrity of the entire case.

“He’s impugning the integrity of a jury … who have sat there solemnly and done their duty, and the judge who oversaw the case and everybody else involved. I think what he did was reprehensible.”

During the trial, Winter had argued that reporting of allegations against Brand by the Times, the Sunday Times and a Channel 4 Dispatches documentary had “poisoned the well”, and rendered it impossible for his client to have a fair trial.

The trial judge, Mr Justice Bennathan, dismissed an attempt by Brand to block his trial from taking place because of negative media coverage.

In a pre-trial argument, Brand, through Winter, had accused the journalists who first exposed him as a sexual predator of being “criminals”, suggesting their reporting in 2023 had made a fair trial impossible and venturing that they may have committed the crime of perverting the course of justice.

But the judge rejected that attempt to stop the trial, ruling that Brand could simply be tried by people who had not seen the previous media reporting.

A Ministry of Justice spokesperson said: “Rape myths have absolutely no place in our courts and victims should never feel as though they are the ones on trial.

“Judges are already able to give directions to counter rape myths but we are going further by changing the law to stop irrelevant sexual history and previous allegations being weaponised against rape victims.

“We will also carefully consider the victims’ commissioner’s proposals as we continue to strengthen protections for victims.”

Brand was convicted of raping a 21-year-old fan in 2009 and sexually assaulting three women in separate incidents between 2004 and 2009.

He is in custody and due to be sentenced on Monday.

#195. The Duo apps are coming, says Apple

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

The early iPhone Duo reviews were a bit hard on Apple’s new foldable for the lack of Duo-optimized apps available at the time of launch. But Apple says things are changing quickly on the software front. The company on Friday announced there are now over 100,000 apps built for the Duo live on the App Store, and it’s seeing thousands more being added to the store every day.

The news was timed alongside the release of over a dozen more notable Duo-ready apps, including Microsoft Word and Outlook, Zoom, sports apps from the NBA and NHL, shopping apps like Etsy and LTK, productivity apps like Goodnotes and Extra, the Halide Mark III camera app, and others. These join the iPhone Duo-optimized apps that were already available, like Netflix, YouTube, Instagram, and WhatsApp.

Non-optimized apps are definitely less than ideal, as they come with a black bar on the side of the screen, which takes up valuable space. Sometimes, they hide important functions under an overflow menu.

Apple’s data seems to suggest that at least some iOS developers may have needed extra time to get their apps ready for its latest form factor. The company said that more than 2,000 app developers attended its over 50 in-person workshops that were hosted across 19 cities worldwide. Here, the developers could use the Duo and work on their apps with Apple’s support.

The Duo has also proven to be more popular than perhaps Apple had planned for, given its high price point. (It starts at $1,999 for the 256GB model.) The device quickly sold out within hours of orders opening up, and now, Duo ship times have shifted into the next year. On its earnings call this week, Apple said it’s “hard to predict” when the Duo constraints leading to those delays will improve.

“It’s been wonderful to see how quickly developers have embraced iPhone Duo, and we’re grateful for their creativity and innovation,” said Susan Prescott, Apple’s vice president of Worldwide Developer Relations, in a statement referring to the 100,000-app milestone. “The enthusiasm has been incredible, and we’re excited to see the pace of updates continue to accelerate as more and more apps optimize for iPhone Duo,” she added.

Other notable apps that have been updated for iOS 27 and optimized for the iPhone Duo include the following: Spotify, CBS, Tubi, BBC iPlayer (U.K.), Yahoo Fantasy Sports, Sportsnet (Canada), Facebook Messenger, Google Chat, ChatGPT, Grok Bot, Microsoft Copilot, Excel, PowerPoint, SAP Concur, monday.com, PDF Expert, Documents, iA Writer, Substack, The Washington Post, Yahoo News, FOX News, Kalshi, Yahoo Finance, Canva, Suno, Tinder, Google Translate, Strava, Alma, Withings, MyFitnessPal, Bevel, Transit, Citi Bike, ParkMobile, Viator, Tesla Robotaxi, Priceline, Kroger, Domino’s, Taco Bell, Untappd, The Weather Channel, FOX Weather, and RainViewer.

Not all these updates have yet to fully roll out, we should note.

Spotify, for instance, is still showing the black bar on the side, and the App Store doesn’t yet say an update is available on our review unit of the Duo. However, given that this list comes directly from Apple, we expect to see these updates soon.

#194. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#191. Three men charged in US with using AI to impersonate CEOs and steal millions

Men allegedly impersonated voices and images of executives to convince employees to send large payments

Three foreign nationals were arrested and charged with using AI to allegedly impersonate corporate CEOs and attorneys to steal over $100m, according to an indictment that was newly unsealed by the US Department of Justice.

According to a press release from the United States attorney’s office for the southern district of New York, three men – Jacob Soall, Sharon Hazan, and Guy Sheetrit – were arrested for using AI to alter and impersonate the voices and images of executives and attorneys at various companies in order to convince employees to send over large payments. Soall and Hazan were arrested in Spain over the summer, while Sheetrit was arrested in the United Kingdom in September. The alleged fraud took place between 2024 and 2026.

With the help of AI, the men allegedly acted as a corporate executive, targeting employees of various companies and telling them they needed their help with sensitive financial transactions. They would then instruct the employees to get in touch with lawyers at a large international law firm. The men would then impersonate a lawyer at these firms and have the employees wire a large amount of money.

The men ultimately stole more than $129m from companies all over the world including in the US, according to the DoJ. The men allegedly stole nearly $5m from a hospital system headquartered in Missouri; about $2.7m from a Rhode Island bank, about $3m from a California hospital system, over $4.6m from a mutual fund headquartered in Massachusetts and about $5m from a pet food company in Florida. The men also stole over $11m from a UK tech company.

The men would direct employees to wire money to bank accounts all over the world including in the United States, Spain, Portugal, Greece, the United Arab Emirates, Singapore, Austria, Slovakia and China. After the money had been wired, the men would covert the money into cryptocurrency “in an attempt to conceal the source, ownership, and control of the stolen proceeds and distributed it amongst themselves and their co-conspirators”, according to the DoJ press release. Soall and Hazan went by several aliases: Soal’s nicknames included “Seba Clarke” and “Rocky Balboa,” while Hazan went by “Superman Cash,” “Fat Boy,” and “Ron.”

In a statement, Jamie McDonald, a US attorney, said artificial intelligence had great potential but that the public should recognize that some may seek to use the technology to pursue criminal goals.

“The alleged scheme orchestrated by Soall, Hazan, and Sheetrit demonstrates how the use of advanced technology can be exploited to commit large-scale fraud,” said James C Barnacle Jr, the FBI assistant director in charge, in a statement. “As criminals continue to weaponize emerging technologies, the FBI remains committed to rapidly adapting our capabilities to identify, investigate, and disrupt those who seek to harm New Yorkers and businesses operating in our communities.”

The men have been charged with conspiracy to commit wire fraud, various counts of money-laundering conspiracy and aggravated identity theft.

#190. Crusoe sells troubled Wyoming data center project to Google

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Crusoe, an eight-year-old, Denver-based data center developer recently valued at $30.9 billion, confirmed Friday it has sold an unfinished data center project in southeastern Wyoming to Google.

Google, which Crusoe had tried and failed to land as a customer for the project, is taking over the development after reportedly raising concerns about its cost and construction timeline under Crusoe’s management.

Crusoe initially announced the project last year with Blackstone-backed infrastructure company Tallgrass, planning to eventually scale it to 10 gigawatts of power capacity.

Google did not disclose how much it paid Crusoe for the unfinished project, but it announced plans to invest $17 billion to develop the data center campus.

The deal highlights how difficult it can be for independent data center developers to deliver massive projects on time and within budget, given the challenges of securing equipment and power. Growing opposition from local communities can further complicate development.

Despite taking over the Wyoming facility, Google hasn’t walked away from working with Crusoe entirely. Crusoe is developing another data center for Google near Amarillo, Texas.

The deal to sell the site to Google was first reported by Bloomberg in June, but the Wyoming setback didn’t stop Crusoe from raising a $3.9 billion round in September.

Crusoe makes money by leasing data center space to customers that bring their own GPUs, by renting out its own GPUs, and by selling compute power used to run AI models, known as inference.

Crusoe’s largest projects serve some of the biggest names in AI. Its campus in Abilene, Texas, is being built in phases for Oracle and OpenAI, and the company is also constructing a separate 900-megawatt campus next door for Microsoft, with its first building expected to come online in mid-2027. Meta is also a customer, and Bloomberg reported that the trading firm Jane Street signed a $13 billion, five-year cloud deal with Crusoe.

Crusoe was founded by Chase Lochmiller, now its CEO, and Cully Cavness, who started it as a cryptocurrency-mining business powered by natural gas that would otherwise have been burned off. In September, alongside its $3.9 billion funding round, the company added three board members, including JB Straubel, the Redwood Materials founder and Tesla board member who invested in Crusoe in 2021. Crusoe later became the first customer of Straubel’s energy-storage business.

#188. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

#187. Thomas Markle, Meghan’s father, charged in Florida in connection to child sexual abuse material

Father of the Duchess of Sussex was taken into custody near Tampa, US prosecutors said

Thomas Markle, the father of Meghan, the Duchess of Sussex, was arrested by US federal agents in Florida on Friday and charged in connection with possession of child sexual abuse material, according to the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI).

Markle, 82, was taken into custody at a senior care facility in Polk county early on Friday, and made a brief initial appearance in federal court in Tampa on Friday afternoon, the Associated Press reported. He was later released with the condition that he be electronically monitored.

A 14-page federal criminal complaint says that agents seized approximately 78 items and alleges that Markle kept child sexual abuse material, including sexually explicit photographs of himself with prepubescent girls, in two Los Angeles-area storage units that he rented for nearly two decades.

An affidavit filed with the ​complaint that led to the arrest stated that two of the images inside the units showed a man identified by a witness as Markle with a nude underage girl.

The document, filed in federal court in California, said the recovered material included dozens of slides, photographs, film negatives and books containing the illegal material. It said the FBI was still reviewing the material.

The director of the FBI, Kash ​Patel, said: “Mr Markle allegedly targeted innocent children and sought to steal their innocence through his ⁠criminal actions. This FBI will use every single resource at our disposal to protect innocent kids – any predator who targets children will have zero ⁠safe haven in this country or anywhere they try to hide.”

Patel thanked FBI agents in Los Angeles and Tampa, and authorities in Mexico, the Philippines, and Marshall Islands that he said were involved in the investigation.

Some details in the criminal complaint are redacted, such as the name of the federal agent who signed the complaint, who states that they are assigned to the violent crimes against children unit in the LA office of the FBI. The case is presented as United States of America v Thomas Wayne Markle.

“Mr Markle is accused of possessing child sexual abuse material, including photographs depicting himself with minor girls,” Bill Essayli, first assistant US attorney for the central district of California, said in a statement released by the US department of justice.

“These allegations are extremely serious, and we will prosecute Mr Markle to the fullest extent of federal law.”

Essayli told the Associated Press that there was no evidence of victims in the US, based on the seized material, but that the investigation was ongoing. He said his office was aware of public reporting that Markle might have dementia.

Markle is charged with one count of possession of child sexual abuse material, and faces up to 20 years in federal prison if convicted.

Efforts by the AP to reach Markle’s court-appointed lawyer for comment were unsuccessful, the news agency said.

Meghan, the wife of Prince Harry, said last month that she was “deeply distressed” about allegations that a collection of child sexual abuse images was found at her father’s former home in the Philippines.

The duchess also revealed that a letter containing “troubling allegations” about her father was sent to police in London in 2018.

She had issued a statement after the Sun newspaper published claims that her half-brother, Thomas Markle Jr, alleged he had found a stash of images belonging to their father at his Philippines residence. He was named as a person of interest in the case earlier this week.

According to the justice department statement, FBI agents interviewed a person last month who was aware that Markle was renting the two storage units in California after the bureau became aware of developments in the Philippines.

Markle rented the units since 2008, it said, with his most recent payments on both made last month by auto-pay.

Photographs produced by the Daily Mail showed Thomas Markle Sr being taken away in a wheelchair, in handcuffs, wearing a black T-shirt and surrounded by agents wearing jackets marked “FBI”. The UK tabloid asked Markle if he was a pedophile and he replied: “No.” Markle did not answer when the newspaper asked if he had ever collected child sexual abuse material.

A spokesperson for the duchess did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Authorities in the Philippines, where Markle ⁠had previously lived for some time, began investigating him last month over allegations he ​possessed child sexual abuse ​images. British police last month said they ​would refer allegations about him to overseas law enforcement agencies after finding that ​claims had not ‌been passed to ​other authorities when ​they were first received.

He has denied the accusations, telling the Daily Mail they were untrue and “sick lies”.

Markle is a retired television lighting and photography director and he did not attend the 2018 wedding of Harry and Meghan, amid acrimony and estrangement between father and daughter. Meghan has since said she decided to distance herself from him to protect her children.

The duke and duchess returned to live in Britain in the summer after six years in the US.

#186. Ilse Aigner tipped by party allies as next German president

German media reports suggest that Bavarian politician Ilse Aigner's chances of becoming the next president are on the up. Long favored by her Bavarian CSU, now it seems Christian Democrats nationwide plan to back her.

Chancellor Friedrich Merz's Christian Democrats (the CDU) and their Bavarian sister party, the CSU, want to nominate a joint candidate to be the next president of Germany when the special "Bundesversammlung" committee that elects one votes at the end of January.

It's long been clear that the CSU favor the current speaker of Bavaria's parliament, Ilse Aigner, for the role.

But on Friday, multiple German news outlets like Spiegel, the Süddeutsche Zeitung and the DPA news agency cited sources in the CDU as saying that party's leadership had also decided on putting Aigner forward.

DPA reported that Merz would officially recommend Aigner as a candidate at a meeting of the CDU's leadership that will convene in Berlin on Sunday and Monday.

On the record, however, party officials would neither confirm nor deny the reports, pointing only to the upcoming meetings on Monday.

If chosen, Aigner would be the 13th person and the first woman to take up the role as post-war Germany's head of state, which is a more ceremonial one with primarily symbolic power, while the bulk of the political and legislative power resides with the chancellor.

She would also be the first CSU politician to hold the office.

Aigner, 61, is currently the speaker of the state parliament in Bavaria.

The youngest of four sisters and the daughter of an electrician, she worked in the family business in her early adult life before going into politics. She's a Roman Catholic, single and has no children.

Aigner began her parliamentary career in the Bavarian legislature in 1994, then moved to the Bundestag federal parliament in 1998, before returning to the Bavarian chamber in 2013.

She spent five years as federal agriculture minister under then-Chancellor Angela Merkel.

She would also be the first CSU politician to hold the office.

Current President Frank-Walter Steinmeier, a heavy hitter for years for the Social Democrats before becoming head of state, is nearing the end of his second five-year term in the role, having assumed the office in March 2017.

The CDU/CSU's junior coalition partners, the Social Democrats (SPD), have also made it clear they would back a presidential bid from Aigner.

"If the CDU steering committee does indeed officially assert itself on Monday and put forward Ilse Aigner, then on behalf of the SPD I can only say that we would welcome this and support it," SPD chairman Lars Klingbeil said at an event in Berlin.

A large committee known as the Bundesversammlung chooses the German president. It will vote on January 30, 2027.

It's made up of all 630 Bundestag parliament members, and another 630 people assigned to represent the various German states — weighted according to each state's share of the national population.

Most of these are regional lawmakers, but municipal politicians and people from other walks of public life are also sometimes given a vote.

The CDU/CSU and SPD combined have a majority of roughly 50 seats in the special committee.

Beyond this, Merz is expected to try to rope in support for Aigner from the Greens, a party that has long championed more female representation at the top of politics and that would not seem to have a realistic chance of propelling a candidate of its own into the role.

This suggests that if Aigner's nomination were to materialize and solidify, she should have fairly good chances of succeeding Steinmeier.

#185. Russell Brand suspected to have attacked at least 10 other women

Met police chief says ‘many more’ victims could come forward, as actor and comedian awaits sentencing

Police are bracing themselves for more victims of Russell Brand to come forward, with at least 10 more women suspected of having been attacked by the disgraced star.

Brand, 51, will be sentenced on Monday to what is expected to be a lengthy jail sentence for rape and sexual assaults after he abused his fame to become a “predator” targeting women, and got away with it for years.

The fallout from Brand’s case saw the government pledge to examine calls to crack down on “rape myths” in trials.

The Guardian also understands that next week the Metropolitan police team that built the case against Brand will start sifting through reports and intelligence about other potential victims.

The Met commissioner, Sir Mark Rowley, said there could be “many more” victims, but declined to say how many, with some intelligence about further victims being third-hand and some further suspected victims reluctant to face the criminal justice system.

Sources say it is feared the number of further victims is at least in “double digits”, with 10 currently seen as a minimum.

The Met police said on Thursday that Brand was under investigation for two further rape allegations. Detectives have sent files to specialist rape prosecutors and are in discussions with them over the material.

Rowley said: “We’ve got two other victims who’ve come forward who want to tell their story, and so two rape cases we’re investigating. I wouldn’t be surprised if many, many more come forward, and we’re open to that.”

Rowley declined on LBC Radio to confirm the new allegations would reach “double figures”, and said: “I’m not going to guess on this, but we’ve seen in the past with high-profile individuals who are brought to justice that it does often bring out a flood of other cases. Let’s see where this one goes.”

Brand’s trial, which spanned claims of attacks from 1999 to 2009, saw women asked by the defence barrister why they had not screamed during attacks.

Since the end of the trial there has been a debate about rape myths. A No 10 source said the prime minister, Andy Burnham, believed calls from the victims’ commissioner, Claire Waxman, for a legal duty that would require judges to issue directions to juries on rape myths and misconceptions, should be examined.

A Ministry of Justice spokesperson said: “Rape myths have absolutely no place in our courts and victims should never feel as though they are the ones on trial.

“Judges are already able to give directions to counter rape myths … we will also carefully consider the victims’ commissioner’s proposals as we continue to strengthen protections for victims.”

Siobhan Blake, the Crown Prosecution Service (CPS) lead for rape and serious sexual offences, said: “The focus must be the actions of the perpetrator and not the behaviour of the victim.

“We know that there is no typical response to sexual assault and myths that victims should scream and fight back are outdated. Some people may freeze or feel unable to speak about it for months or even years, but this is not a barrier to justice.”

Brand now faces years of imprisonment and the growing prospect of a fresh trial over further allegations.

He was convicted on Thursday of raping a young fan at his luxury London townhouse in 2009, continuing despite her pleas for him to stop. He had picked her out of a crowd. He was also found guilty of sexually assaulting three women in separate incidents at a radio station, a recording studio and a bar.

Victims are expected to tell of the impact his crimes had on them in court on Monday.

Brand became an international star, acting in Hollywood films, and in latter years an online influencer, but was dogged by whispers and allegations about his danger to women. He was accused of treating women as a “disposable commodity” and a “means to an end” to satisfy his voracious sexual appetite.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

The government says it is committed to reducing violence against women and girls by half.

Burnham told Times Radio: “It’s horrifying what Russell Brand has done. And I’d firstly like to praise the bravery of the women, the victims in the case, who’ve come forward and had the courage to speak out.

“But yes, the journalists too, who supported those who had been abused by Russell Brand. So all of them deserve our praise for having that courage to come forward.”

Later on the BBC, Burnham was asked about a statement of support for Brand in 2014 as the star faced media criticism, and said: “Well, over a decade ago, and all I can say is I had no idea of the full extent of what he was doing or what he went on to do. His crimes are horrific.”

Burnham told Times Radio he will look at making changes to rape trials so that they “do not leave the woman feeling like she is on trial”.

He said: “We need to constantly look at the way rape trials are conducted to make sure that they do not leave the woman feeling like she is on trial in court, as has been the experience of people in the past … we need to encourage more women to come forward to report rape.”

The comedian Katherine Ryan, who warned of a “predator” in the entertainment industry before Brand’s trial, said she felt relief at his conviction.

“They came forward despite Russell Brand having fame, power, money and a legion of fans ready to attack them,” she said.

“I will never be someone who, like, dances on graves. There has been some justice done today. There’s a level of relief, but there’s grief alongside relief.”

Bear Grylls, who baptised Brand when he converted to Christianity in 2024, said the star’s faith seemed genuine and added: “It is right that justice has now been served. I can’t imagine how hard this time has been for the victims, the heroes of the day.”

Brand was cleared of two further charges of rape and one allegation of attempted indecent assault.

Brand’s victims told how he “snapped” – changing from charming to nasty – as he raped and sexually assaulted them before gaslighting them into believing nothing was wrong.
