Separate actions also take place outside fence and across Europe in backlash against impact of AI boom

Activists have stormed the site of a Microsoft datacentre being built in Amsterdam, as the backlash to the social and environmental harms of the AI boom spreads across Europe.

Protesters from Geef Tegengas, a Dutch direct action group, clashed with police while breaking into a construction site at Westpoort, Amsterdam, where some members climbed a crane, lit flares and tied helium balloons to equipment.

The action comes amid Europe-wide resistance to the rapid expansion of energy-hungry AI, with more than 100 groups involved in a two-day series of protests to demand moratoria on the construction of new datacentres.

The break-in at the Dutch site, where protesters hung banners objecting to Microsoft’s services to the Israeli military and US Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE), was separate from the pan-European “Stop Dirty Data Centres” campaign, which targeted the same site but whose affiliated members stayed outside the perimeter fence.

Police said in a statement to NOS, a Dutch public broadcaster: “The police attempted to prevent [the occupation] and arrest protesters, but were unable to prevent it and force was used.

“An officer was slightly injured during the protest.”

Protesters from both groups said police used force and teargas to dispel the activists trying to enter the construction site. Organisers and local media reported hundreds of protesters on each side of the fence.

Smaller protests took place in the centre of Bristol, in the UK, at a datacentre in Berlin, Germany, outside the Polish parliament in Warsaw and at the main square in Zagreb, Croatia. Further protests are expected on Saturday.

Stop Dirty Data Centres, a coalition of independent groups that include national branches of Greenpeace and Friends of the Earth, said it had not coordinated or promoted the direct action that took place in the Netherlands, but that is was “understandable” communities would turn to a range of tactics when they were unable to meaningfully participate in decision-making.

“Big tech and government are racing ahead with large-scale datacentre development despite significant public concern about the impacts on local communities, natural resources and other industries,” said Owen Espley, campaign coordinator. “Many datacentre developments are planned with limited transparency or scrutiny, while communities across Europe are being shut out of decisions that will shape their local environments and futures.”

The ecological fallout of the datacentre boom has alarmed some climate scientists, while growing frustration with AI’s societal impact – from energy bills and job losses to the role of tech companies in undermining democracy – has increased public anger. In the US midterm elections this week, nearly every proposed moratorium on datacentres on the ballot was approved.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Roos Molenaar, an activist from Geef Tegengas, said the struggle against big tech was inseparable from the fight against oppression and fascism. “We won’t accept Amsterdam being used for the creation of AI slop and the facilitation of genocide,” said Molenaar. “This datacentre is gulping up our drinking water to make the rich even richer.”

A spokesperson for Microsoft said it was aware of the demonstration and that its first concern was the wellbeing of everyone at and around the site. “We understand that people feel strongly about the project, and we respect their right to express those views peacefully. However, we do not condone any actions that are unlawful or that put people’s safety at risk.”

A spokesperson for Pure Data Centres, the site owner, said it respected the right to peaceful protest but that entering a live construction site was unlawful and dangerous.

“Our immediate priority is everyone’s safety, and we respectfully ask those on site to leave safely and engage with us in dialogue about the development and any concerns they may have,” a Pure DC spokesperson said.

Governments across Europe have supported the building of AI datacentres to boost tax revenues and catch up with the breakneck deployment of computing power in the US and China, with some framing the AI race as a matter of national security.

But datacentres have emerged as a flashpoint for public anger over issues from environment and energy to human rights and democracy. Last week, an investigation by the Guardian and Investigate Europe found evidence of dangerous conditions for some of the workers building them.

#116. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#114. Death a ‘distinct possibility’ in collision running and face-slapping competitions, inquiry hears

Impacts in emerging contact sports are akin to coward punches and cannot be made safer with regulation, neurologist says

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Collision running and face-slapping competitions have similar impacts to that of a coward punch or “king-hit”, with death a “distinct possibility”, a concussion expert has told a parliamentary inquiry.

A New South Wales inquiry is examining whether emerging competitions such as Run Nation, in which two players run at each other and collide at high speed, and Power Slap, where players take turns slapping each other across the face, should be considered combat sports to better regulate the safety of participants.

But neurologist Dr Rowena Mobbs, an expert in concussion and chronic traumatic encepalopahty (CTE), told the inquiry she did not think there was “any form of modification” that would make the sports safer.

“Collision running and Power Slap are worse than eye-gouging and should be deemed a king-hit, for death is a distinct possibility,” she said.

A 19-year-old died in New Zealand last year after suffering a critical head injury playing a tackling game believed to have been inspired by the viral “run it straight” challenge, of which Run Nation’s organisers have claimed their competition is a safer version, turning rugby league “hit-ups” into a sport.

The inquiry was established in August. In September, a NSW supreme court judge ruled Run Nation was a combat sport, upholding a cancellation notice for a Sydney event that month from the Combat Sports Authority (CSA) of NSW.

The event has been rescheduled for 28 November after the authority’s medical advisory board approved its permit. Power Slap has yet to host an official event in Australia.

The chair of the CSA, Darren Kane, told the inquiry on Friday he would prefer to see new combat sports regulated, with mandated ambulance and medical support staff.

“If it was a regulated event, there’d be all these systems in place, and the people would have accreditations, they would have done concussion training, there would be doctors mandated at the events,” he said.

“Whereas you have these unregulated things, it’s pot luck.”

Run Nation’s organisers have said their events include on-field doctors and paramedics.

The inquiry did not hear from organisers of either competition, but received submissions from medical experts, bareknuckle boxing and grassroots combat sports groups.

Petero Taremwa, the founder of Get That Guap (GTG) fight club, which hosts mixed martial arts, kickboxing and boxing fights without a promoter’s licence, said the cost of participation was a “serious barrier” to fighters, while organisers faced significant costs from “permits, insurance, venues, and medical services”.

“We’re not here to dispute the importance of regulation. We’re here because we want a realistic pathway to comply,” he said.

Taremwa said there had been no significant injuries at GTG events, which “always had a nurse who is also our DJ, who looks at the fighters right after the fight”.

Under questioning from the inquiry chair, Labor MLC Sarah Kaine, Taremwa said he did not think he knew better than the CSA, but that the cost of on-site ambulances required under regulation was unreasonable for an entry-level organiser.

In her evidence, Mobbs said brain diseases in participants in football codes and traditional combat sports resulted from “subclinical concussions or silent events … when they add up to probably tens of thousands if not hundreds of thousands”.

But she said the stronger impacts experienced in collision running and face-slapping competitions meant a lower number of impacts could “trigger off a neurodegenerative disease, for example, later in life”.

Mobbs said she did not recall treating any patients with injuries from collision running or Power Slap.

She likened the potential impact to that of a coward punch or “king-hit”, because participants could not defend themselves and strikes came “right in the key area where you have most impact”.

The inquiry is expected to report back in December.

#109. The Victorian Liberals’ campaign bus was supposed to be a reset. Instead it became the symbol of a rough week

Crashing into a shop awning ended the first outing of the Jess Express – but the opposition’s electoral momentum will be harder to stop

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Jess Wilson has barely put a foot wrong since becoming Victorian Liberal leader a little under a year ago. That was until this week, which ended with her campaign bus reversing into a small business.

The bus was launched on Friday, a few days earlier than planned, in what appeared to be an effort to reset Wilson’s relationship with the Spring Street press pack.

A day earlier, journalists had been left waiting for an hour as Wilson announced a $100m investment in medical research to an enthusiastic room of researchers at the Burnet Institute. But when it came to questions she stayed for just four minutes and 39 seconds – answering a single question from each of the nine journalists present before cutting the press conference short.

The pack had been keen to press Wilson on Labor’s claim that the Coalition’s election policies would cost $6.35bn more than they had disclosed.

The treasurer, Colin Brooks, had earlier spent 20 minutes taking journalists through a glossy booklet Labor titled “Jess Wilson’s Dodgy Costings”, which detailed 30 Coalition promises it claimed had not been costed and dozens it said the opposition had underquoted.

It was an interesting stunt given Labor has its own cost blowouts and financial problems – including net debt that is forecast to reach $199bn within four years. But they are not the only ones questioning how the Coalition plans to deliver a surplus by 2032 while making $8bn worth of election promises, offering tax cuts and vowing not to cut frontline services.

The Liberals have made much of their credentials as better economic managers than Labor and Wilson, as shadow treasurer, should have been prepared to stay and defend her own numbers.

The moment played into Brooks’s suggestion earlier in the day that the Liberals “don’t want to be subject to the scrutiny of having journalists … asking questions during the day”.

“They’re thinking they’re going to coast home to this election result, and that’s treating the Victorian people with contempt,” he said.

A similar thing happened last week, when Wilson’s team cancelled a press conference amid confusion over the Coalition’s plan to “pause and review” the multibillion-dollar Suburban Rail Loop project.

Then there is the debate brouhaha. Both News24 and Nine had requested a debate involving Wilson, the Labor premier, Ben Carroll, and the One Nation leader, Warren Pickering – given the latter is now commanding about 20% of primary votes in most polls.

Wilson has refused, instead insisting on a traditional one-on-one format with Labor, arguing that only the two major parties could form government. Carroll wants Pickering to be included – seemingly keen to keep alive the idea of a Liberal-One Nation coalition, despite polling suggesting the Liberal-National Coalition should be able to win government in its own right.

Last week, News24 seemed to resolve the impasse by also inviting the Greens leader, Ellen Sandell.

But no agreement has been reached on Nine’s proposal. Wilson issued a statement on Thursday saying she had agreed to debate Carroll next week. This time Labor refused, claiming Nine has offered Pickering the opportunity to appear in a separate 10-minute segment during the same broadcast.

The Liberals reset on Friday, unveiling the “Jess Express” campaign bus.

Wilson was joined by her team and recruits from Canberra – including her close friend and federal Liberal senator James Paterson, and Kane Silom, who has taken leave from his role as communications director for the federal Liberal leader, Angus Taylor, to work on the Victorian campaign.

She chatted with journalists on the bus before it arrived at a Bentleigh cafe, where she made coffees and served brunch before a press conference, this time clocking in at more than 20 minutes.

Pressed again on her party’s costings, she said voters would have to wait until after early voting begins to see them.

“We cannot release our costings until the government has released its pre-election budget update,” Wilson said.

She batted off questions about the Liberals’ latest election commitment – to sell $1.3bn worth of government-owned land. Labor says this is more “dodgy costings” as it includes land earmarked for first home buyers, and a plot where radioactive cow carcasses were buried that would cost millions to clean up.

“Those costs have been factored in as part of the independent costings by the Parliamentary Budget Office,” Wilson said.

Then it was back on the bus and to the Centre Road shops – one of which became the victim of the Jess Express.

But perhaps there’s a glimmer of hope. Despite the damage, the store owner wasn’t too fussed, provided his awning is repaired.

Nor was it enough for him to rule out voting Liberal – he said he was still undecided.

It will take a little more to stop the Coalition momentum at this stage of the campaign.

Benita Kolovos is Guardian Australia’s Victorian state correspondent

#107. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#103. US midterm results spark fears in Israel over future of two countries' ties

Israel and the US have been staunch allies for decades

As the US midterm election results rolled in, long-held Israeli assumptions about the transatlantic alliance began to shatter.

"For us in Israel, this will be a generational sea change," wrote one prominent journalist in Israel's biggest-selling newspaper, right-leaning Yedioth Ahronoth. "The [prime minister, Benjamin] Netanyahu government sowed the wind and reaped the tsunami."

Israel's opposition leaders have lined up to blame their prime minister, with one saying the US results showed "just how toxic Netanyahu has become" among both Democrats and Republicans.

The prime minister himself has so far remained silent.

"Israel's political establishment understood intellectually that things have been shifting. They have not understood how much and how quickly," one US official with knowledge of the situation told me.

Israel's relationship with the US was a campaign issue in races across America.

Many Democratic candidates were sharply critical of Israel's actions in Gaza and the occupied West Bank, and the costs of the joint US-Israeli war on Iran was a key issue for many voters.

The US president himself blamed the Iran War for the Republican rout â a war that Israel's leader is widely seen as having promoted to him.

And US Vice-President JD Vance cited the US relationship to Israel as one of four key reasons voters had deserted the party, hammering home the message that the relationship is now seen as a political problem by its key ally.

"That might be even worse than what happened in the midterms," Israeli political and security analyst Yaakov Katz told me. "Because the Democrats we knew about, now [Vance] is pointing the finger and saying: 'Our voters want us to change our position on Israel' â and Israelis don't fully understand where this is going."

What the US midterm results mean for the Middle East

The question plaguing Israelis now is what this means for Israel's security and influence.

The sense of staunch US military and diplomatic support had remained unshaken in Israel despite growing anger among US voters, President Donald Trump's public insults to Netanyahu, and differences over Israel's actions in Iran and Lebanon.

Israel's prime minister continually boasted of his closeness to Trump, describing his "true friendship" and "unwavering support".

And just nine months ago, Israelis watched their armed forces wage war on Iran hand in hand with the US military, as the "unprecedented" coordination of the two allies became a daily mantra among military spokespeople and politicians.

So Trump's unexpected statement during a Fox News interview this week that he would stop $3.8bn (3.4bn euros; Â£2.8bn) in US military aid to Israel two years earlier than planned, caused shock here â not so much for the loss of aid as for what the move said about Israel's status as a diplomatic partner.

Israel's president said the announcement came as "a bolt from the blue".

"Nobody knows anything," was the headline in a report by Yedioth Ahronoth, adding that officials were still struggling to understand whether Trump's remark was serious, two days on.

The Israeli prime minister's office responded to the news several days later, saying that the shift from US aid to partnership showed "the strength and sophistication" of the bilateral relationship.

US military aid is less than 10% of Israel's annual military budget. But Yaakov Katz, author of several books about Israel's defence and security, said Trump's announcement was the tip of a much larger question around future funding, arms sales and joint military projects with Israel. All of this could be slowed or stalled by a Democrat-controlled Congress, even without the two-thirds majority it would need to overrule Trump.

"Everything that Israel used to get in a simpler, easier process will now potentially be much more complicated, and Israel has to be prepared that this is a new reality," he told me.

"Our entire Air Force is American-made military planes, we're dependent on American spare parts and American weapons. The last three years have shown us how dependent we are on American missile-defence systems. [If] those don't show up at the same pace, that's a real difference â that changes your calculation of what you're able to withstand if you go to war."

Katz believes the midterms could also translate into tougher demands on Israel over its actions in Gaza and Lebanon, and a requirement not to undermine an agreement with Iran.

"Something will change in the US-Israeli relationship, but it's a question of scale," said the US official I spoke to, who was not authorized to speak on the record. "It means shifting to [being] willing to criticise Israel when it's warranted, but the bedrock fundamental relationship will still be there."

In an interview on Israeli television, the US ambassador to Israel Mike Huckabee signalled little shift so far in core American positions, including on Israel's occupation of the West Bank, describing the sanctions imposed by other countries as "irrational" and counterproductive.

Israel's ambassador to the UN, Danny Danon, told another Israeli channel that Trump would be in the White House for the next two years, and that Israel could "feel good" about the US veto in the UN Security Council â something Washington has repeatedly used in the past to block resolutions that target Israel.

"But we have to pay attention to the voices â not only in the Democratic Party but also the Republican Party â criticising Israel," he said. "It will be harder for us."

Trump endorsed Netanyahu in Israel's elections

Questions over its key alliance have come at a particularly sensitive time for Israel, less than two weeks after its own parliamentary elections left neither government nor opposition with a clear majority. Discussions are now taking place with the country's president, who will decide whether Netanyahu or his centrist rival, Gadi Eisenkot, will get the first chance to form a governing coalition.

"Under my leadership, we will renew and deepen that [US] partnership, strengthen the bipartisan foundations that have long sustained it," Eisenkot said in a social media post.

Another member of his opposition bloc, Yair Lapid, said: "What do we do?"

"We go back to the rule that worked for us for seventy years, and which broke in 2015 when Netanyahu went to speak against [US president] Obama in Congress. Israel doesn't choose sides in America. We work with everyone."

Netanyahu's speech to the US Congress in 2015 is seen as a defining moment in Israel's relationship with the US, when the Israeli leader appealed over the head of America's Democratic president to urge Republicans in Congress not to back a nuclear agreement with Iran.

Since then, Netanyahu has increasingly anchored the alliance in Trump's Republican party, even as alienation among Democrats has grown.

The US official I spoke to said that, before the midterms, Netanyahu could have improved the relationship with Washington by getting rid of his two far-right ministers.

"I think everyone understands now it's bigger than [that]," he said.

How much the new tone in transatlantic relations will play into the task of building a new government in Israel is being closely watched.

Some government supporters argue it has increased the need for political stability, and that Netanyahu provides much-needed continuity and a deep knowledge of the US and its leaders.

"We cannot afford to be dragged into endless political disputes and election campaigns," said one opinion writer in Israel's largest-circulation daily, the right-wing Israel Hayom.

Whoever heads Israel's next government, its American ally may demand more than rhetoric.

In the noisy debate around what this will mean for Israel, the silence of its prime minister â once the whisperer of Washington's elite â is perhaps the most telling voice of all.

Can Democrats in Congress end the war in Iran?

#101. Anti-fire ant eradication activist convicted after using legal arguments ‘similar to sovereign citizens’

Legal expert compares Trevor Hold’s failed defence to ‘tried and tested argument’ used by sovereign citizen movement

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Queensland leader of a group opposing the eradication of fire ants in Australia has been convicted of obstructing government officers, after a magistrate threw out his argument that the commonwealth-funded program is illegal.

Trevor Hold was fined $1,500 but a conviction was not recorded. Hold has described himself as an “ultrapreneur”, a mental health counsellor, an organic cattle farmer and the chief executive officer of the anti-program group the Australian Advocacy Group – and as self-employed.

He was convicted last month of two counts of obstruction for attempting to prevent fire ant treatment at his property, in what is believed to be a Queensland first.

His organisation and others held a series of well-attended meetings last year across south-east Queensland to advise residents on what the group called their legal rights to refuse or prevent access to their properties to the program.

Under Queensland’s Biosecurity Act, landholders must permit program officers to access their property to treat fire ants, which are a declared priority pest.

But the organisations advised landholders to use a provision in the act allowing them to obstruct an officer if they had a “reasonable excuse” to refuse access, a provision in the Biosecurity Act.

On 16 April at about 9.20am, Hold prevented officers from entering his property in Adare in the Lockyer Valley, west of Brisbane. The property was in the treatment zone and due for its annual treatment.

The eradication program had informed Hold in advance that it would treat his property that day. A small group of supporters gathered to film and yell at them while they did so, according to court documents.

The scene was later described as “an increasingly rowdy and crowded and dynamic situation” in the Ipswich magistrates court, which heard Hold’s charges last month.

He was arrested by Queensland police after about 40 minutes for obstructing them by not allowing a gate to be opened. He was then un-arrested. Despite being warned again, he was again arrested after another interaction with program officers.

Hold made three arguments to the magistrate, Leanne Scoines, in his defence.

He argued that he was intimidated by the police and the program officers and “withdrew his consent for their attendance”, describing himself as “frazzled”.

“It was put to Mr Hold that he was not frazzled, and that he had said on three occasions, as heard in the body-worn camera footage, that ‘this is the Trevor Hold show’,” Scoines said.

Through his lawyer, solicitor Beatrice Ludwig, Hold argued that the police had failed to take into account his “reasonable excuse” and were therefore acting outside their powers.

Hold based part of his argument on emails he sent to the Department of Primary Industries in December 2024 and January 2025, notices the magistrate described as “unilateral”, “quasi-legal documents … seeking tacit approval by non-response”. The magistrate found they were of no legal effect.

Finally, he challenged the validity of the program, arguing that the prosecution had not proven it was properly authorised under the Biosecurity Act, Scoines said.

All three arguments were dismissed and the magistrate found that the officers were entitled to proceed on the basis that the DPI officers were exercising powers conferred on them by the Biosecurity Act.

“I am not satisfied that Mr Hold was a credible or liable witness on the critical issues in dispute,” she said.

A police prosecutor told the court “In my respectful submission it was clear he was actually enjoying this incident”.

Hold has appealed against the judgment to the district court.

On his website, Hold advertises fundraising efforts for his defence, and also a mooted class action lawsuit against the program.

David Heilpern, a Southern Cross University law professor, compared some of the arguments used by Hold to sovereign citizen cases he used to judge as a magistrate in the NSW Northern Rivers region, particularly his claim that he hadn’t consented to fire ant treatment.

“That is a tried and tested argument in terms of many of those who are on the fringe of or deep into the sovereign citizen movement; that you have a right to remove consent from the operations of various laws,” Heilpern said.

“Whether you consent or don’t consent is completely immaterial under the biosecurity legislation.”

#100. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#97. With Stephen Miller pick, Trump readies for a battle

In the aftermath of the Republican Party's sweeping defeat in Tuesday's midterm elections, Donald Trump said he would be willing to work with the newly ascendent Democrats in Congress.

On Friday, however, he elevated Stephen Miller, his bare-knuckled political operative and longtime adviser, to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff.

Far from an olive branch to Congress, Miller's promotion could be a sign that the president is girding for political battle by installing a partisan firebrand in the top White House job.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our president and defend this magnificent republic," Miller posted on X, adding that he was "honored beyond words" to be Trump's pick for the job.

One of the most powerful positions in Washington, the chief of staff is gatekeeper to the president, agenda-setter in the White House and chief negotiator with Congress and other outside political interests.

"Stephen Miller's been with me since the beginning," Trump said in comments to reporters after announcing the move on his Truth Social website. "He gets it."

Watch: Trump says he appointed Stephen Miller âto make a changeâ

Miller is best known for being the driving force behind the Trump administration's aggressive immigration enforcement policies, which has included ramping up funding for enforcement operations across the US.

He made headlines when he called Alex Pretti, who was shot and killed by federal immigration officials during a massive deportation operation in Minneapolis, a "domestic terrorist" and an "assassin". He would later walk back those words, saying federal agents "may not have been following" proper protocol.

He has also reportedly played a leading role in the US military operations in Venezuela and against suspected drug trafficking operations in the Caribbean and eastern Pacific, which critics have condemned as extra-judicial killings.

Miller joined Trump's political operation early in his 2016 presidential bid as a strategist and incendiary warm-up speaker at his raucous campaign rallies.

He was one of the few senior White House staffers to persevere through the entirety of his first term. He stayed loyal to Trump during his political exile following attempts to overturn his ballot-box defeat in 2020 failed. He then returned in the influential role of deputy chief of staff for policy in Trump's second term.

Wiles, who co-managed the president's successful 2024 re-election campaign, developed a reputation as a cool professional who avoided the White House staffing upheavals, dramatic policy reversals and personality clashes that typified Trump's first presidential term.

The president, however, began souring on Wiles in recent months, as his political standing in the US declined, culminating in Tuesday's electoral rout. He also reportedly chafed at news stories indicating that those in Wiles' orbit were landing lucrative consulting jobs through their connections to the president.

Trump names Stephen Miller as new White House chief of staff

'Trump's psyche': The aide driving president's most controversial policies

What has Stephen Millerâs role been in Trumpâs second term?

While Trump praised Miller's effectiveness as a manager on Friday, working with Congress could prove to be a challenge.

It is difficult to overstate how disliked Miller is by most Democrats â and some Republicans.

"Stephen Miller is a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands," Democratic House leader Hakeem Jeffries wrote on X. "His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor."

Earlier this year, Miller posted on social media that James Talarico, who had just secured the Democratic nomination to run for Senate in Texas, was "their first transgender Senate candidate".

The Democratic Party offered a terse, obscenity laced reply, telling Miller to shut up.

Now Miller will have to negotiate with a US Senate that includes a newly elected Talarico as one of its rising stars, after an election in which polls found that 58% of voters believe the administration's immigration enforcement actions had "gone too far" and Hispanics decisively turned against Republican candidates.

On Wednesday, in a social media post attempting to explain the Republican midterm defeats, Vice-president JD Vance - who is an early frontrunner for the Republican 2028 presidential nomination - wrote that he was "open to the idea that we should change enforcement- by focusing more on businesses that hire illegal labor, for instance".

That now seems less likely with an immigration hardline taking the White House helm.

Thom Tillis, the North Carolina Republican senator who is retiring at the end of the year, has been particularly vocal in condemning Miller. He has called him "incompetent" and "the architect of some of the dumbest proposals from the Trump White House".

Other Republicans, however, have sung his praises.

"Stephen Miller has been a true conservative warrior since his days as a Senate staffer, a staunch defender of law and order against illegal invasions and leftist lawlessness, and a steadfast fighter for President Trump for over a decade," Utah Senator Mike Lee posted on X after Trump's announcement.

At a time when congressional Democrats are promising to launch new investigations and oversight into the Trump administration, it appears that a "warrior" and a "fighter" is the kind of person the president wants to have running his White House.

#96. When a single vowel stands in the path to victory, the stakes could not be higher for these spelling bee champions

Primary school students bested 200,000 competitors across New South Wales to reach the Premier’s Spelling Bee finals in Sydney

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Competing in a spelling bee is not for the faint of heart. Sometimes, the difference between victory and loss is one incorrect vowel and you’re undone, months of training reduced to nought.

At a small theatre hall in Sydney’s west on Friday afternoon, 34 primary school students sit stony-faced on a brightly lit stage, knowing this pressure only too well.

All have made it past about 200,000 students who competed in spelling bees across 1,070 public schools in New South Wales. The finalists earned their place at this year’s state competition after winning online regional finals in August. They represent the top 0.03%. And they’re not here to mess around.

Five are competing for a third time, having progressed to the last stage in the junior competition. Among them are year 5 student Wafiq Ayyash from the Hunter region, who won the junior state title in 2024, and year 6 student Bobby Ivaneza from the inner west, who was runner-up the same year.

Ewan Mattick from the state’s central west, Caleb Borg from the Riverina, and Alesha Kahfandi from the Hunter have also qualified for the state finals on three occasions. This isn’t their first rodeo.

The format is relatively simple. Contestants begin with “seen” words that they’ve been able to practise at home, before facing “unseen” words testing their broader skills and composure. Each round is sudden death.

To kick off, they’re introduced to the crowd by the host, ABC presenter Rae Johnston, with colourful details.

Archie Griffith likes the Parramatta Eels and his dachshund puppy. He doesn’t like his dad’s jokes. Ivaneza, who is wearing a bright red sweatband, admires Nicola Tesla, a Serbian-American engineer who died in 1943, for his genius. Ivaneza’s favourite food is camembert.

The students are as diverse as the schools they come from. Some 23 speak a language other than English at home, and 18 have travelled from outside Sydney.

Seven go down in the first round, stumbling over obscure words like “tourmaline” and “malapropism”. Another seven are out in round two – but you try spelling “lepidopterist” at home.

In the next four rounds, 13 students fall. Mattick nails “centurion” and gives a thumbs up to his family. Come round seven, Kahfandi begins to pray.

“Pernicious” and “sagacious” bring two more students down. And then there are five: Afeef Ahmed, Ivaneza, Kahfandi, Caroline Wang, and Maximilian Lumley, all in year 6. Two are returning finalists.

In round eight, they all spell their words correctly, though Ivaneza, usually breezing through with confidence, restarts on “whimsical”. Then, in round nine, he’s asked to spell “unserviceable” and omits the second “e”.

Ivaneza, visibly devastated, slumps into his seat and places his head in his hands. The remaining students spell their words correctly, and Ivaneza tearily returns to his family in the crowd and the comforting embrace of his father. The heartbreak is palpable.

Then, in an instant, after Ahmed correctly spells “immemorial”, the remaining three competitors fall. Kahfandi misses “antepenultimate”, Wang gets tied up by “parmigiana” and Lumley is one letter off getting “genealogy”. Ahmed has won.

“We are in an interesting situation,” Johnston says. “We need to play for a runner up.” Wang goes out straight away after being dealt the word “grandiloquent”. But Kahfandi and Lumley appear to be in lockstep. For the next five rounds, they’re either both correct or both wrong.

“We’re just going to keep on going,” Johnston declares. It begins to feel that the audience will grow old in this theatre, steadily working its way through the entire Macquarie Dictionary.

But then, Kahfandi misses the word “vociferous”. Lumley approaches the microphone and awaits his fate. His word is “apocalypse” and, after a deep breath, he nails it.

The crowd bursts into cheers, and Lumley returns to his seat and stares reflectively at the ground, looking like he has endured a war. His grandmother, Sue, is bursting with pride. “It was so stressful,” she says, “and he looked so calm.”

“He’s worked so hard. Some of those words, I didn’t even know what they were. We’re very proud.”

Kahfandi is proud of herself, too. “It was very tense in the room, there was a lot of pressure on everyone,” she says. “I feel a bit disappointed I didn’t get second and I was a bit tired after all those rounds, but getting third place is also really good.”

Ahmed is surprised he’s won, but his mother isn’t. She’s been helping him train for months – half an hour every night before bed.

“He reads a lot,” she says. “I knew he would win; he was three-time champion at school.”

Asked how he felt when he found out he’d won Ahmed says, at first, he “didn’t realise”.

“But then when they said we had a winner, I felt happy,” he says. “The runners-up words were harder than the ones I got.”

#92. This man refused life-saving treatment because of his religion - days later the rules changed

Isaac Valdez had an epileptic seizure and his condition deteriorated rapidly

In August this year, 26-year-old Isaac Valdez had an epileptic seizure while browsing in his local library, causing him to convulse and strike his head against the ground.

He lost consciousness. Later, in hospital, it transpired Isaac had hit his head with such force it had led to a brain haemorrhage.

Treatment was seriously complicated by the fact that, as a Jehovah's Witness, Isaac carried a "No Blood" card, an indication that he would not accept treatment involving any form of blood transfusion because of his religious beliefs.

As a result, a procedure to drain fluid from Isaac's brain did not take place.

But his family, from Waxahachie, Texas, were encouraged by the fact that he regained consciousness and even started to talk again. He was able to confirm that he stood by his "no blood" beliefs.

The family's relief at his apparent improvement was short-lived. Isaac slipped into a coma and his condition worsened rapidly.

A neurosurgeon told Isaac's family the only way to save his life was an emergency operation, but that it was one that required blood transfusion.

His family took the decision to abide by Issac's wishes as a lifelong Witness not to accept such a procedure.

Isaac - who his twin brother Jacob Valdez describes as an intelligent man who loved volunteering and helping the elderly - died on 26 August 2026 when doctors turned off his life support.

Jacob's "no blood" card which is like the one carried in Isaac's wallet

Just 23 days later, the Jehovah's Witnesses' Governing Body announced a seismic change in teaching that would, in effect, have given Isaac the religious green light to have the life-saving treatment he had refused.

The change suddenly means the vast majority of medical procedures using donated blood are no longer prohibited to followers. Now, decisions on whether to accept such treatments are to be left to individuals.

Jacob, already dealing with the death of his twin, was angered at the Jehovah's Witnesses' leadership for not coming to their conclusion sooner. He told BBC News he believes Isaac could well still be alive if they had.

Jacob (right) believes his twin Isaac could still be alive if the change in teaching had come sooner

The original rule on blood transfusions dates back to 1945, towards the very end of a war during which the use of blood transfusions had been increasingly widespread. The leaders of Jehovah's Witnesses announced that their believers were not permitted to accept transfusions.

This was based on their interpretation of Bible verses from both the Old and New Testaments prohibiting the consumption of blood, including one from the Book of Acts that instructs believers to abstain from blood, from food offered to idols, and from things that had been strangled.

So, why has the Jehovah's Witnesses' Governing Body, based in New York state, now decided to change the rules, 81 years after introducing them?

It comes after years of pressure from both within and outside the faith, but that is not how leaders of the faith describe the decision.

"The change is basically the application of Bible principles to modern medicine and its methods," says the Jehovah's Witnesses international spokesperson, Paul Gillies, who described it as both a fresh look at scripture and at medical advances.

Jehovah's Witnesses international spokesperson Paul Gillies says the ban on transfusion of whole blood remains but blood components can now be used

We meet Gillies at the Jehovah's Witnesses UK headquarters set in 85 acres of Essex countryside. It is a vast modern complex where hundreds of volunteer followers of the faith live and work without pay.

He insists the basic teaching has not changed and that the transfusion of whole blood is still prohibited. However, for decades, most transfusions have not used whole blood, instead using the specific components of blood that are needed by the patient.

The change is being justified by permitting the use of blood components, like plasma and red blood cells and platelets, in the huge numbers of medical procedures that were previously banned.

Gillies says the Bible references "abstaining from blood" but not "various components that could be extracted from blood".

He says the Governing Body felt it their duty to avoid scriptural "overreach" and only apply what was specifically described in the Bible.

But that explanation has stunned many and led them to ask if it was not a huge overreach 81 years ago to ban blood transfusions in the first place, when transfusions are not specifically mentioned in the bible.

Others are then also asking why it has taken so long to come to this landmark decision, given that transfusions that do not use whole blood have been the norm for decades.

These are questions Jacob asked as soon as the ban was lifted, just days after a memorial service for Isaac.

Jacob, who had already left the Jehovah's Witness faith he was brought up in, texted prominent figures from Isaac's congregation in Texas after Isaac's death. The messages are full of angst, and painful to read.

He asked them to consider "the part you played in encouraging so many people to sacrifice their life for something that was never God's will".

Most did not reply, but one "elder" responded saying he could not understand why Jacob was so angry and fixated on the policy shift.

This all further infuriated Jacob, who particularly wants the faith's decision-makers to be accountable for all the lives lost.

"I want to ask them if they could apologise directly, I would like not to have a deflected answer, just straightforward apology," he says.

It is extremely difficult to say how many Jehovah's Witnesses, among the estimated nine million followers of the faith worldwide, died each year having refused treatment involving blood transfusions. But research in one medical journal in 2016, The Obstetrical & Gynecological Survey, put the figure at around 1,100.

"We're sympathetic to their feelings, and if individuals have lost family members and are concerned about some of these questions, they deserve to be heard compassionately," says Gillies.

But there was little clarity about why the change did not come earlier.

"Why has it taken this time? Well, I guess because it takes time, it doesn't necessarily mean that there is some magic change that's taken place," says Gillies.

And when asked, Gillies deflects calls for the leadership to apologise, saying that many lives had, in fact, been saved because faithful Jehovah's Witnesses had not been exposed to blood contamination scandals.

He also insists that even after the new edict, some Jehovah's Witnesses could still decide they did not want to accept a transfusion, and that may have been the case even if the edict had come years ago.

Indeed, the BBC spoke with Alex Ball, 58, a Jehovah's Witness from West Sussex, who says in 2012 a doctor told him he would not survive the chemotherapy he needed to treat his blood cancer unless he also had a transfusion.

"I wanted to adhere as closely to the scriptures as possible when I was sick, and I think I did that well."

With help from what is called "a Jehovah's Witness Hospital Liaison Committee", Alex found an American consultant who could perform a treatment to boost Alex's blood cells without the need for any products from someone else. His UK consultant agreed to treat him using the new method and it worked.

More than a decade later, Alex says he would make the same decision again, despite the change in blood policy that would have permitted the transfusions he had been told he needed.

It is not just receiving blood which has been an issue.

Elisha Cummings, 50, a Jehovah's Witness from Trinidad and Tobago, was unable to donate blood to her aunt, who died, because of the rules.

Elisha Cummings was advised by an elder not to donate blood to her aunt Eva who died

They also cost her her marriage too, with her husband of 10 years leaving the faith and divorcing her after it came to light that he had had a blood transfusion as a child, something that was looked down upon in her Witness community.

"Since the blood policy change, I have not been myself," she says. "It left me in a sort of depressed mode."

"How many houses did I go to trying to convince people about the blood doctrine? And you're coming now and it's something totally different," Elisha says.

Elisha says her beliefs as a whole have been shaken, yet she feels reluctant to leave the faith, partly because it is what provides her with her community and she fears isolation.

In the past, those who disagreed with "blood doctrine" were ostracised or felt like they needed to leave the faith. Will they now be able to come back?

"Any individual that wants to come back to the faith is warmly welcomed," says Gillies, speaking of those who had left the faith because they had accepted a transfusion for themselves or a relative.

"If they would like to become one of Jehovah's Witnesses again and would like to speak to the elders, then they're very welcome to do so."

The pressure on the Church of England to ditch its slavery reparations plan

We thought Gen Z had started going to church in droves. But the truth is more complicated

#89. The young Somali woman changing how the world sees Mogadishu

Somalia has the longest coastline in mainland Africa

As the sun rises over the Indian Ocean, waves lap onto the golden sands while palm fronds rustle in the cool sea breeze. Welcome to... Mogadishu.

This is the image that one young Somali woman wants the world to have of her home city - in contrast to the one of suicide attacks and young men wielding AK-47s that many people currently associate with Somalia's capital.

Sucdi Abdul Fattah Abdul Khader is among a growing number of local guides taking visitors beyond the headlines to explore Mogadishu's beauty spots, historic sites and culture.

Her favourite spot is Jazeera beach, where visitors can enjoy the sea before heading to nearby sand dunes.

"It's one of the tourist attractions where we bring our guests. It's a nice place," says the 23-year-old.

The dunes, she adds, remind some visitors of the Sahara.

But her tours are about more than scenic views. She also takes visitors to the fish market, where they discover the variety of marine life available along Somalia's extensive coastline and to parts of the city that reflect its history as a busy Indian Ocean trading centre, with the influences of both its Ottoman and Italian former rulers.

Visitors can also sample Somali food, including camel meat, samosas and traditional tea.

Sucdi says many arrive in the city with expectations shaped by years of hearing about the insecurity.

"Mostly, they are surprised by how beautiful the country is and how welcoming people are," she says.

At the fish market, she says, visitors are particularly impressed by the variety of fish species.

Sucdi Abdul Fattah Abdul Khader wants visitors to experience the "beautiful, welcoming side of Mogadishu"

Guiding tours runs in Sucdi's family. Her father worked in the profession for over three decades, taking journalists, photographers and visitors from different countries around Somalia.

He introduced her to the business nearly three years ago.

"I started this job as a hobby, but it has become something that I like and am passionate about," she says.

She now works alongside her father and a team of guides, building on his experience while using social media to reach a wider audience.

She shares videos of her tours on TikTok, offering people outside Somalia a glimpse of places they may never have imagined visiting.

She is also considering producing content in English to reach audiences who do not speak Somali.

A wide variety of fish are available in Mogadishu's markets

Tourism remains a relatively new field in Somalia, she says, with only a handful working as guides in Mogadishu.

The business is seasonal, but Sucdi says this year has been particularly encouraging, although numbers are still fairly low. Having five tourists a week is considered a success but she is confident that numbers will grow.

However, she says many visitors are still concerned about security.

Since the collapse of Somalia's central government in 1991, Mogadishu has endured decades of civil war, destruction and displacement. Much of the city centre was reduced to rubble, and basic services and infrastructure largely broke down.

But following the withdrawal of the Islamist militant group al-Shabab from most of the city in 2011, and the establishment of the federal government in 2012, Mogadishu has undergone a gradual transformation.

Returning members of the Somali diaspora, local entrepreneurs and investors helped fuel a construction boom, with new hotels, restaurants, businesses and residential developments reshaping the capital.

Today, Mogadishu is a growing commercial hub, although insecurity and the legacy of conflict remain part of everyday life.

About 10,000 tourists visited Somalia in 2024, a 50% increase on the previous year, according to the country's tourism department.

Many visitors ask about their safety before travelling and often encounter security checkpoints around the city.

But she says their experience on the ground is generally positive.

Lido beach is one of the most popular places for people to relax in Mogadishu

"What they heard from the news and everywhere else is not the same as what they see in the country," she says.

Although some question whether Mogadishu is safe enough for a young woman to be working as a tour guide, Sucdi is determined to continue.

She wants visitors to experience a Somalia that is rarely seen.

"I would want them to see the beautiful, welcoming side of Mogadishu, and not the side that they heard from the media, which is explosions and bombs," she says.

Somalia has Africa's longest mainland coastline, stretching more than 3,000km (1,860 miles) along the Indian Ocean and Gulf of Aden.

Its beaches and sand dunes sit alongside historic coastal cities such as Mogadishu, whose culture reflects centuries of Indian Ocean trade.

For some tourists, that contrast between the country's reputation and what they experience can be striking.

American travel content creator Chris Reese, who goes by the name Authentic Travelling, has visited Mogadishu twice.

His first trip, almost a year ago, was a solo visit. He enjoyed it so much that he decided to return with a group of friends, who had seen videos of his visit on social media.

"I had such a positive experience - I really enjoyed seeing the country for the first time - that I opted to bring back a group of my friends," he says.

What stands out for Chris is the city's coastal setting, its food and atmosphere.

"It is such a beautiful tropical place. I love the vibe, the weather and the food," he says.

The sand dunes near Mogadishu remind some visitors of the Sahara Desert

During the group's visit, they enjoy lunch overlooking the Indian Ocean before heading out to explore the beaches and sand dunes.

"The desert is beautiful and picturesque. It is also amazing that it's barely a 30-minute drive from Mogadishu," he says.

Before his first trip, Chris says, much of what he knew about Somalia came from international news coverage.

"If you only read the news reports about Somalia, you would be scared to visit. And honestly, that's what I saw in the media before I came, mostly negative headlines."

But visiting the country challenged those perceptions.

"Coming here, on my very first trip, showed me that there is so much more to this country than what is shown in the headlines," he says.

Romanian Mihaela Patap says she prefers to visit new countries without preconceived ideas

Mihaela Patap, from Romania, loves Somalia's natural beauty.

Although she has spent only a few hours in Mogadishu, she has already visited the sand dunes and taken in the Indian Ocean coastline.

"The desert was so beautiful to see. I have also enjoyed the beach, and the ocean. It is inspiring," she says.

Having travelled to Somalia from neighbouring Ethiopia, she says she has been struck by the people's friendliness.

"I don't see anything dangerous. I feel safe and the people are warm," she says.

An experienced traveller, Mihaela says she prefers to visit new countries without having preconceived ideas.

"I travel the world to see new places, to see countries, to see the differences. I don't take a picture in my mind to compare the reality with. I'm just expecting the new to come to me," she says.

She hopes to share her experience and encourage others to visit Somalia.

"I'm going to post my pictures and say good things about what I'm experiencing. I'm going to promote it," she says.

She is continuing a family profession while building a business in an industry with few women and using digital platforms to reach audiences far beyond Mogadishu.

"Somalia still has a long way to go before tourism becomes a major industry," she says.

"Through my tours, I am giving visitors an opportunity to experience its landscapes, our culture and people beyond the familiar negative headlines."

For her, every visitor is another opportunity to tell a fuller story of Mogadishu.

#88. Ben Roberts-Smith collaborates with gym business run by influencer that sold ‘war crimes’ pre-workout supplement

Zero Limits supplements is co-owned by rightwing podcaster and fitness coach Chris Katelaris, also known as ‘Big Chocky’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Ben Roberts-Smith has collaborated with a gym supplements company run by a rightwing influencer which recently sold a pre-workout product called “war crimes”.

Roberts-Smith, one of Australia’s most decorated living soldiers who is currently awaiting trial on war crimes charges, has recently launched an apparel brand, Epic 1 Two, producing T-shirts and gym wear.

In October, he also appeared in a social media video collaboration between his brand and Zero Limits supplements, co-owned by the rightwing podcaster and fitness coach Chris Katelaris, also known as “Big Chocky”.

Roberts-Smith, Katelaris and others were filmed during a gym workout, with on-screen graphics reading “Zero Limits Supplements x Epic 1 Two”. Both brands were labelled “collaborators” on the video, which is also published on the Instagram grids of both brands.

Months earlier, Zero Limits had repeatedly posted online about a product titled “war crimes”, an energy supplement in the flavour “kill or capture cola”. Its label depicted a hooded captive on a chair in a room with an Australian flag on the wall, and a tall figure wearing a balaclava and military-style clothing.

In a video on 26 July, Katelaris promoted the product while holding a tub at the gym.

“You wanna drink out of a prosthetic leg? You wanna re-enact a Sparta kick? You wanna execute your workout?” he said.

“War crimes, it’s the pre-workout for you.”

Zero Limits, which says it is “veteran owned and operated”, does not currently list the “war crimes” product on its website, and Roberts-Smith is not shown endorsing, referencing or using that particular product.

The former soldier has praised another product from Zero Limits, posting an Instagram story – since shared by the brand – from Epic 1 Two’s Instagram page of himself drinking a separate pre-workout drink.

Roberts-Smith gave the brand a “massive shout out” and told followers to “get on their website and take a look”, sharing a link to its website. Roberts-Smith also last week appeared on the Zero Limits podcast, hosted by another founder of the supplements company of the same name.

Roberts-Smith was in April arrested and charged with five counts of war crime – murder, in relation to three incidents stemming from his service in Afghanistan in 2009 and 2012.

He has denied the charges, saying in a statement after his arrest: “As I’ve always maintained: I categorically deny all of these allegations … I will be taking this opportunity to finally clear my name. I’m proud of my service in Afghanistan.”

The three incidents allegedly involved Afghan nationals being shot dead by Roberts-Smith, or a subordinate under his command, while he was present, the AFP commissioner, Krissy Barrett, said in April.

Roberts-Smith was previously found in a defamation suit – using the civil standard of the balance of probabilities – to have murdered unarmed civilians while serving in the Australian Special Air Service Regiment (SAS) in Afghanistan.

The federal court found to the lower civil standard that Roberts-Smith kicked Afghan man Ali Jan in the chest, sending him falling backwards over a cliff before he landed on the ground. The court found that Roberts-Smith then ordered another soldier to shoot him dead.

Roberts-Smith was also accused in media reporting of shooting an unarmed prisoner who had a prosthetic leg. The leg was later used as a drinking vessel by soldiers.

Zero Limits, Katelaris and Roberts-Smith were contacted for comment.

In a statement via a lawyer, Zero Limits did not respond to specific questions, only answering: “The products are great, make sure you check them out.”

In a statement via the same lawyer, Katelaris also did not respond to specific questions, but said: “If you thought this was wild, wait until next week’s campaign drops.”

Katelaris lists himself as a “co-owner” of the supplements brand in his Instagram bio. He also promotes a range of merchandise under his own name in support of Roberts-Smith, including “I stand with BRS” T-shirts, hats, hoodies and stubby coolers.
